# ROUND 2/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The mission requires robots to be **stationary before the switch** and **moving after the switch**. The switch is signaled by a GREEN wall lighting up.

Key observations:
- **Champion (score 10032)**: Simple "Stop" (B=1) with 1 state. This works great before the switch but costs points after.
- The floor is uniformly GRAY, so floor color conditions are useless.
- The GREEN signal (color 2 in the perception system) is the only cue for the switch.
- Ideal behavior: Stop until GREEN is seen → then move (Exploration).

The best strategy: Start in Stop state, transition to Exploration (or GoToColor green) when green is perceived. The camera can detect the green wall (color 2).

**Machine design:**
- State 0: Stop (B=1) → transition to State 1 when green color perceived (C=7, l=2)
- State 1: Exploration (B=0) → stay moving (maybe small prob to go back)

The champion is just Stop forever. We need to beat 10032 by moving after the switch. The switch happens at step 400-600, run lasts 1200 steps. If we stop for ~500 steps (cost ≈ 500×20=10000) and move after (~700 steps, cost=0), total ≈ 10000 which is close. But the champion gets 10032 which means it stops ALL 1200 steps × 20 robots / some factor... actually 10032 ÷ 20 robots ÷ 1200 steps ≈ 0.42 fraction moving? No — champion stops always, so before switch it gets 0 penalty per step per robot, after switch it gets 1 per step per robot.

Score = (switch_time × 0) + (1200 - switch_time) × 20 ≈ (700) × 20 = 14000... but champion scores 10032. So switch_time ≈ 1200 - 10032/20 ≈ 1200 - 501 = 699. OK so robots that stop always score ~10032.

To beat this: Stop before green, move after green. Score = switch_time×0 + detection_delay×20 + 0. Detection delay is key — if they detect green quickly, score ≈ 0 after switch time. This should give much lower scores!

Let me design candidates around Stop→move-on-green, with variations:

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 1 --cle0 0

(Survival of champion)

Now variations:

1. **2-state: Stop→Explore on green** (main exploitation target)
2. **2-state: Stop→GoToColor(green)** 
3. **3-state: Stop→Explore on green, back to stop if no green**
4. Various probability/parameter tweaks

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.9 --l0x0 2 --s1 0 --rwm1 50 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 7 --p1x0 0.05 --l1x0 2

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 1.0 --l0x0 2 --s1 0 --rwm1 30 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.01

And more...

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Key observations from round 2:

1. **Champion is still just Stop (B=1), score 9929**. The 2-state Stop→Explore-on-green machines score ~10500-11000, which is *worse* than pure Stop. This is surprising.

2. Why does Stop→Explore-on-green perform worse? The green wall detection via camera (C=7, color 2) apparently doesn't work reliably enough — robots may not see the wall from their position, or they transition too early/late. When they do move, they might not all see green simultaneously, causing partial transitions that still penalize.

3. The best 2-state machine (score 10548) uses p=0.9 for green detection and rwm=30 — slightly lower than p=1.0. This suggests the transition sometimes fires at wrong times (false positives?).

4. **The pure Stop machine is hard to beat** because: if the switch happens at step 500, the post-switch penalty is 700×20=14000, but the champion scores ~9929, meaning robots aren't all stopping perfectly — there's some movement noise. Actually wait, ~9929/20 robots = ~496 steps of "moving" on average, which is roughly 1200-500-204 = the post-switch time minus detection time. Hmm.

Actually re-examining: pure Stop scores 9929 which means ~496 robot-steps of movement (post-switch). But pure stop means 0 movement... unless the score formula counts stationary robots POST-switch as cost. YES! After switch: moving robots cost 1 per step. Before switch: stationary robots cost 1 per step. Pure Stop: costs nothing before switch, costs 20×(1200-switch_time) after. With switch at ~500, that's 20×700=14000. But score is 9929, so switch_time ≈ 1200 - 9929/20 ≈ 703. OK so switch is late on average.

The ideal machine: Stop before switch, all move after switch → score ≈ detection_delay × 20 ≈ very low if detection is fast.

But 2-state machines scored ~10500 > 9929. Why? Because some robots incorrectly move before switch (false positives from green detection), costing 1 per step pre-switch for those robots. The camera might see other colored objects or noise.

**Key insight**: The green wall is the only green thing. Color 2 = green. But robots might see it even before the switch if they're near that wall? No — the wall only lights up AT switch time. So false positives shouldn't occur before switch.

Wait — maybe the issue is that after the switch, not ALL robots can see the green wall simultaneously (some face away, are far, etc.), so they transition slowly. The robot that transitions to moving still pays moving cost (0) but the delay in detection means they're stationary post-switch (costing 1 per step).

Actually: the pure Stop costs 20×(1200-switch) post-switch. The 2-state machine costs: pre-switch false positives × 20 + post-switch delayed-detection × 20. Since 2-state scores higher (worse), there must be some false positives OR the delayed detection hurts more than expected.

Let me try: **lower transition probability** to reduce false positives, and ensure robots keep moving once they start. Also try **higher rwm** to move faster/more randomly.

Also try: Stop → GoToColor(green) which would directly approach the green wall — but that also moves, costing score post-switch... wait no, moving AFTER switch costs 0, moving BEFORE switch costs 1. GoToColor(green) is fine if triggered only after switch.

Let me also try: what if we add a second transition from Stop state that fires with tiny probability (like 0.001) to serve as a fallback in case green is never seen? And keep the explorer moving.

Actually the best strategy might be: use a **very low probability** random transition from Stop as a baseline, combined with the green detector. But that might hurt more.

Let me try varying parameters more aggressively around the best performers:
- Best: p=0.9, rwm=30, back-transition p=0.01 → 10548
- 2nd best: p=1.0, rwm=30, back-transition p=0.01 → 10578

Try: p=0.95, rwm=30, lower back-transition (p=0.005 or 0.001)
Try: p=0.9, rwm=50, p=0.005
Try: p=0.85, rwm=30, p=0.01
Try: p=1.0, rwm=30, p=0.005 (reduce accidental return)
Try: p=0.9, rwm=20, p=0.01

Also explore: 3-state with Stop→Explore-on-green, Explore→GoToColor-green (so they find the wall after switch), GoToColor→stop-never.

Also: what if the moving state has NO back-transition (or near-zero)? That keeps robots moving after green is detected.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The pattern is very clear now: **pure Stop (score ~9929-10057) consistently beats all 2-state machines (score ~10500-10900)**. The green-detection approach doesn't help enough.

Why? The 2-state machines (Stop→Explore on green) score worse than pure Stop. This means the robots are paying a penalty BEFORE the switch somehow, OR the detection is too slow/unreliable after the switch.

Let me reconsider the scoring:
- Before switch: penalty for stationary robots = 1/step/robot
- After switch: penalty for moving robots = 1/step/robot

Wait — I had this backwards! Let me re-read: "Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is **stationary**." and "From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is **moving**."

So BEFORE switch: stationary = bad, moving = good!
AFTER switch: moving = bad, stationary = good!

**Complete reversal of my understanding!**

So the optimal strategy is:
- **MOVE before the switch** (to avoid penalty for being stationary)
- **STOP after the switch** (to avoid penalty for moving)

The pure Stop machine scores ~9929 because it stops all 1200 steps. Before switch (~500 steps): 20 robots × 500 steps = 10000 penalty (for being stationary). After switch: 0 penalty (stationary is fine). Total ≈ 10000. This matches!

The 2-state machines (Stop→Explore on green) score WORSE because:
- Before switch: they're stopped (paying 1/step/robot) - same as pure stop
- After switch: they start moving when they see green (paying 1/step/robot for moving) - worse!

So the ideal machine:
- **State 0: Move (Exploration)** - before switch
- **State 1: Stop** - after switch, triggered by seeing GREEN

The transition should be: Moving → Stop when green is perceived (C=7, color=2).

Score would be: before switch = 0 (moving is good), after switch = detection_delay × 20 (brief penalty while transitioning). This should give VERY LOW scores!

Let me design these machines:
- State 0: Exploration (B=0) → transition to Stop when green perceived
- State 1: Stop (B=1) → stay stopped (tiny back-transition probability or none)

The back-transition from Stop should be near zero to avoid moving after switch.

For the "moving" state, I should also try Attraction/Repulsion which move differently than Exploration.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Excellent progress! The champion (score 8721) is the 2-state Explore→Stop-on-green machine with a **dual transition** from Explore: (1) green perceived with p=0.9, and (2) random with p=0.001. The Stop state has a tiny random back-transition (p=0.001).

Key insights from round 4:
1. **Explore (rwm=30) → Stop on green** is the winning topology
2. The **dual transition** in state 0 (green detection + tiny random fallback) beats single-transition variants (8721 vs ~9789)
3. Lower rwm (30) beats higher (50, 70) — robots turn less, move more consistently
4. p=0.9 for green detection seems better than p=1.0 or p=0.8
5. The Stop state back-transition (p=0.001) is essential but should be tiny

**What to exploit:**
- Champion: rwm=30, p_green=0.9, p_random=0.001, back=0.001
- Try varying p_green (0.85, 0.9, 0.95), p_random (0.0005, 0.001, 0.002), back (0.0005, 0.001, 0.002)
- Try rwm=25, rwm=35
- Try adding a second transition from Stop (green disappears → go back to explore? No, that's bad)
- Try 3-state: Explore → Stop-on-green, with a "confirmed stop" state

**What to explore:**
- 3-state variants with intermediate states
- Different p_random values (the fallback transition is interesting — it causes occasional random transitions to Stop even without green, which might help if some robots never see the green wall)
- Try adding neighbour-based transitions

The random fallback (p=0.001 per step) means after ~1000 steps, a robot that never sees green will transition to Stop anyway. With switch at ~500-700 steps and 700 remaining steps, this helps stragglers.

Let me try:
- Vary p_random: 0.0005, 0.001, 0.002, 0.003
- Vary p_green: 0.85, 0.9, 0.95, 1.0
- Vary rwm: 20, 25, 30, 35
- Vary back-transition: 0.0001, 0.0005, 0.001
- 3-state with extra confirmation

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (score 8164) is a 3-state machine:
- State 0: Explore (rwm=30) → goes to State 1 (Stop) on green (p=0.9) OR random (p=0.001)
- State 1: Stop → goes to State 1 (itself via destination index 1... wait, n1x0 destination is 1, but with 3 states, leaving state 1, destination D=1 targets state 2 (the others are [0,2], so D=0→state0, D=1→state2). So Stop→State2 on random p=0.001
- State 2: Stop → goes to State 0 (Explore) on green (p=0.9)

Wait, let me re-read the champion:
- State 0 (Explore rwm=30): transition 0→ dest=0 on green p=0.9 (from state 0, others are [1,2], D=0→state1), transition 1→ dest=1 on random p=0.001 (D=1→state2... wait)

Actually with 3 states, leaving state 0, the others are [1, 2]: D=0→state1, D=1→state2.

So State 0 transitions: D=0 (→State1) on green p=0.9, D=1 (→State2) on random p=0.001.
State 1 (Stop): transition D=1 (from state1, others=[0,2], D=1→state2) on random p=0.001.
State 2 (Stop): transition D=0 (from state2, others=[0,1], D=0→state0) on green p=0.9.

So the structure is:
- State 0: Explore → State1 (Stop) on green, → State2 (Stop) on random
- State 1: Stop → State2 (Stop) on random (essentially stays stopped, occasionally shuffles to state2)
- State 2: Stop → State0 (Explore) on green (this is bad! after stopping due to green, if green disappears... wait, after switch green stays lit)

Hmm, State2→State0 on green detection means: if a robot ends up in State2 and sees green, it goes BACK to exploring! That seems counterproductive. But it scored best...

Maybe the green wall stays lit permanently after switch, so State2 robots immediately go back to Explore. But wait — that would create an oscillation. Unless the green wall is only visible to nearby robots.

Actually the real insight might be: State2 is rarely entered (only via the p=0.001 random from State1), and when green is visible (post-switch), State2→State0 sends them back to explore. But since green IS always visible post-switch, State2 would immediately go to State0... this creates a loop: State1→State2→State0→State1 (on green). 

The key: State0→State1 on green (p=0.9 per step), so once they see green they quickly go to State1 and stay. The State1→State2→State0→State1 loop happens at p=0.001 per step, so very rarely.

The benefit might be: before switch, State2 (Stop) robots go to Explore when they accidentally detect something that looks green (false positive?), keeping them moving. But there should be no green before switch.

OR: The 3-state structure's advantage is that the random fallback from State0 goes to State2 (Stop) rather than State1 (Stop), and State2→State0 on green keeps robots in explore when green is not seen yet. This is complex.

Let me focus on exploitation of the champion's structure and explore variations:

Key parameters to vary:
1. rwm in state 0 (try 15, 20, 25, 30)
2. p_green in s0 (0.85, 0.9, 0.95)
3. p_random in s0 (0.0005, 0.001, 0.002)
4. p_random in s1 (0.0005, 0.001)
5. p_green in s2 (0.85, 0.9, 0.95, 1.0)
6. Try 4-state extensions

Also: the 2-state with back=0.0001 scored 8314, very close to champion. Let me try even lower back transitions.

For exploration: try 4-state machines, or different movement in state 0 (GoToColor different colors before switch?).

## Reasoning

Clear trends from round 6:
1. **Lower rwm is better**: rwm=15 (7470) > rwm=20 (7658) > rwm=25 (7883) > rwm=30 (~8088). Try rwm=5, 10.
2. **3-state structure** with the specific topology beats 2-state variants
3. **p_random=0.001** beats p_random=0.0005 (8647 vs 7470)
4. **p_back in State1=0.001** is good; p_back=0.0001 slightly worse (8079)
5. **p_green=0.9** seems optimal; 0.95 and 0.85 both worse
6. The champion structure: Explore(rwm=15) → Stop on green(0.9) or random(0.001); Stop→Stop2 on random(0.001); Stop2→Explore on green(0.9)

The key question: why does lower rwm help? Lower rwm = less turning = more straight-line movement. This keeps robots moving (good before switch) and covers more ground to find the green wall.

Let me try rwm=5, 8, 10, 12 to find the sweet spot below 15.

Also: the 2-state with rwm=20, back=0.0001 scored 7735 — close to 3-state champion. Try 2-state with rwm=15, back=0.0001.

For the 3-state champion, also try:
- Different p_green in s2 (0.95, 0.85)  
- Different p_random values (0.0015, 0.002)
- rwm combinations: rwm0=15 with slight variations

## Reasoning

Very clear trend: **rwm=5 is best** (6263), rwm=8 (6514), rwm=10 (6956), rwm=12 (7016). The trend strongly suggests even lower rwm might be better, but rwm=1 is the minimum. Let me try rwm=1, 2, 3, 4.

The champion is rwm=5 with the 3-state structure. Key parameters:
- p_green=0.9 (both s0 and s2)
- p_random=0.001 (both s0→s2 and s1→s2)

Also interesting: the 2-state with rwm=10, back=0.0001 scored 7119 — decent. Try 2-state with rwm=5, back=0.0001.

For exploration, I should also try:
- Varying p_random around 0.001 for champion structure
- Varying p_green around 0.9
- Different p_back in s1

The dominant factor is clearly rwm. Let me aggressively explore rwm=1,2,3,4 while keeping other parameters fixed at champion values. Also try slight variations of p_green and p_random.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Excellent results! rwm=1 and rwm=2 are tied at the top (5391.93). This is the minimum rwm value, so we can't go lower. The topology is very well established.

Key observations:
1. **rwm=1 and rwm=2 are equally best** - we've hit the minimum, can't improve rwm further
2. **p_random=0.001 in s0** is slightly better than 0.002 (5391 vs 5443 for rwm=3)
3. **p_green=0.9** remains optimal
4. **p_back in s1=0.001** is optimal (0.0005 and 0.002 both slightly worse)
5. The 3-state structure is definitively better than 2-state

Now I need to focus on:
1. **Fine-tuning p_random** around 0.001 for rwm=1 and rwm=2 (try 0.0005, 0.0008, 0.0012, 0.0015, 0.002)
2. **Fine-tuning p_green** around 0.9 (try 0.88, 0.92, 0.85, 0.95)
3. **Fine-tuning p_back in s1** (try 0.0005, 0.0008, 0.0015)
4. **Exploration**: try 4-state extensions, adding a second green-detection transition in s1

The champion: rwm=1, p_green=0.9, p_random=0.001, p_back=0.001, p_s2_green=0.9

For exploration, I should think about whether adding more states or transitions could help. The 3-state machine's state 2 goes back to explore when green is seen — this seems counterproductive (post-switch, all robots that stopped and see green go back to exploring). But it works well, possibly because the structure creates a useful equilibrium.

Maybe trying: state 2 with NO back-transition (stays stopped forever once in state 2), using a 4-state where state 3 is also Stop with no transitions... but we need at least 1 transition per state.

Actually, let me think about what makes rwm=1 better. With rwm=1, robots barely turn — they essentially drive straight. This means they cover ground efficiently and are more likely to encounter the green wall's field of view. The camera is 360° in e-puck, so turning rate doesn't affect detection... but movement pattern affects area coverage. Very low turning means robots drive in nearly straight lines, bouncing off walls, covering the arena well.

For the last 2 rounds, I should mostly exploit the champion and make small adjustments:

## Reasoning

This is the final round. Key findings:

1. **p_random=0.002** (in s0) is now better than 0.001 (4787 vs 5127)
2. **p_random=0.0005** is much worse (5952)
3. **rwm=1 and rwm=2** are tied at the top
4. **p_back in s1=0.001** remains optimal
5. **p_green=0.9** in both s0 and s2 remains best
6. The champion: rwm=1, p_green=0.9, p_random=0.002, p_back=0.001, p_s2_green=0.9

For the final round, I should:
- Keep the champion
- Try p_random around 0.002: try 0.0025, 0.003, 0.0015, 0.0018
- Try p_back variations: 0.0008, 0.0012
- Try p_green variations in both states: 0.88, 0.92
- Try combining rwm=1 with slightly higher p_random
- A few exploration attempts with 4-state or different structures

The gap between p_random=0.002 (4787) and p_random=0.001 (5127) is large — about 340 points. The gap between 0.001 and 0.0005 (5952) is also large. So 0.002 is a sweet spot. Try 0.0025, 0.003, 0.0017.

Also worth noting: scores below 4787 are theoretically possible. The minimum score if all robots detect green instantly after switch and stop: ~0 penalty after switch + 0 before = 0. But detection delay is the limiting factor.

Let me focus tightly on parameter tuning around the champion:

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
